    All products, sums, and tests in this definition are bounded finite constructions once B is fixed.
We next prove that the cutoff is exhaustive on this entire initial segment, so that a failed bounded
test selects a negative instance.
                                                                                             2
Lemma 17.7 (Size of a graph-witness search). For 1 ≤ n ≤ M∗ and 0 ≤ t < 2n , there is a
program of Definition 17.1, of size less than k, which terminates exactly when Q(n, t, j) holds for
some positive j. On termination it outputs the least such j. The program contains neither B nor
any oracle instruction.


                                                            174
Proof. Fix this particular pair n, t. Consider the following program schema, where the first two
right-hand sides are arithmetic terms for the specified binary numerals and Q is expanded as in
Definition 17.4:
                              v1 := the numeral for n;
                              v2 := the numeral for t;
                              v3 := 1;
                              while ¬Q(v1 , v2 , v3 ) do v3 := v3 + 1;
                              v0 := v3 .
Binary sequencing makes this a program of Definition 17.1. Each test of Q is bounded and therefore
terminates. The loop tests the positive integers in order, proving the claimed termination and
output properties without assuming P (n, t). Writing the numerals by binary Horner expressions
                                                            2
uses only 0, 1, addition, and multiplication. Since t < 2n , the two numeral terms cost at most
100(n + 1)3 nodes, with ample allowance for their fixed register targets.
    The bounded formula templates and the rest of the program schema are fixed independently of
n, t. Their expansion can be counted directly. Use 2 = (1 + 1), replace A ⇒ B by ¬A ∨ B, replace
A ⇔ B by (¬A ∨ B) ∧ (¬B ∨ A), and use binary conjunctions and one binder for each successive
quantifier. Substitute every abbreviation separately, without shared subexpressions or loop unrolling.
Counting a variable occurrence initially as one gives:

                 Clause                     Nodes    Variable occurrences    Binders
                 Symmetry                      53                      26             2
                 Bottom condition             173                      68             4
                 Triangle condition           240                      92             4
                 Order reflection             198                      72             4
                 Mask and connectivity        717                     281            13
                 Full Q                      1394                     542            28
The last line adds four conjunction nodes and the outer existential: its binder and the seven-node
           1+1
bound nj        contribute nine nodes with the quantifier itself. Renaming the expanded binders
consecutively as v100 , . . . , v127 avoids all capture; the bound of each quantifier is renamed in the
preceding environment. Only v1 , v2 , v3 remain free. Every node then costs at most 128, so Q costs
at most 128 · 1394 = 178432. Outside the two numeral assignments, the rest of the program costs
29: initialization costs 6, the while and negation nodes cost 2, increment costs 11, final output costs
6, and the four sequencing nodes cost 4. In particular the following much larger allowance bounds
the entire program:
                                                                         4
                                  100(n + 1)3 + (2 · 106 )204 < 2(M∗ +10) = k.                  (17.17)
The second summand exceeds 178461. For the strict inequality, M∗ + 1 ≤ 2M∗ bounds the first
summand by 23M∗ +7 , and 2 · 106 < 221 bounds the second by 24284 . Since M∗ > 109 , their sum is
                                                4
less than 23M∗ +8 , which is less than 2(M∗ +10) .

                                                                                          2
Lemma 17.8 (Uniform Busy Beaver witness cutoff). If 1 ≤ n ≤ M∗ , 0 ≤ t < 2n , and P (n, t)
holds, then
                    R(Tn,t ) ⇐⇒ (∃j ∈ {1, . . . , B}) Q(n, t, j).                 (17.18)
In fact every representable such table has a witness of size strictly less than B.




                                                 175
Proof. For a representable table, Lemma 17.5 makes the program in Lemma 17.7 terminate with
a witness size j. Its size is less than k, so the definition of B gives j < B. Conversely, any j ≤ B
satisfying Q(n, t, j) supplies a representation by Lemma 17.5.
Theorem 17.9 (Finite description of a minimum example). The bounded formulas (17.13)–(17.16),
with the ordinary unevaluated Busy Beaver constant (17.1), specify the full finite order table of a
nonrepresentable lattice L0 of minimum cardinality. Its cardinality is the integer a0 in (17.15), and
                                              7 < a0 ≤ M∗ .
This is a finite order description and a proof of minimum cardinality; it does not supply a numerically
evaluated value of that cardinality.
Proof. By Lemma 17.8, for every index in I the bit zn,t is one exactly when the table is a
nonrepresentable lattice. By Theorem 16.7, some such bit is one. Let (n∗ , t∗ ) be the first index for
which it is one. The product in (17.14) is one at that index; at each later index it contains the zero
factor 1 − zn∗ ,t∗ , and at each earlier index the factor zn,t is zero. Hence exactly one wn,t equals one.
Formula (17.15) gives a0 = n∗ and t0 = t∗ , so (17.16) is precisely this lattice’s full order table. It is
nonrepresentable.
    Every nonempty lattice of cardinality smaller than a0 has an order table among earlier indices in
I. Its selection bit is zero and its lattice predicate is true, so the cutoff lemma makes it representable.
Thus L0 has minimum cardinality among all finite nonrepresentable lattices. The represented range
through seven elements in Theorem 3.4 gives a0 > 7, and its index gives a0 ≤ M∗ . Nothing in
this argument evaluates B: all its uses concern the outputs of the terminating programs in its
definition. The result is therefore the claimed finite description with an exhaustive bound, rather
than a numerical enumeration.
    The constants and entries above are unambiguous in the standard natural numbers. Their
definition supplies no ordinary computable procedure for evaluating the Busy Beaver constant, no
evaluated decimal value of a0 , and no conventional small diagram of the minimum lattice. Those
would be stronger forms of identification than the finite description proved here.
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Part (i) is Theorem 2.2; Part (ii) is Theorem 15.3. The finite description
and its minimum-cardinality property are proved in Theorem 17.9. The strict lower bound of seven
follows from Theorem 3.4. Part (iv) is Corollary 15.4.
